みずほ経済 EXPRESS

中国が住宅販売モデルを転換へ 
～不動産不況は脱却の兆しなお見えず～

調査部 主任エコノミスト 月岡直樹  
080-1069-6684 naoki.tsukioka@mizuho-rt.co.jp 
■ 中国政府が新規則を発表。住宅は予約販売から完成後販売にシフト 
中国政府は8月28日、住宅販売制度と住宅ローン管理に関する新規則を発表した。新規の住宅不動産
プロジェクトについて完成後販売の方式を促すほか、未完成物件の予約販売における住宅ローンの実
行時期を物件引き渡し後に変更する。住宅の予約販売制度が不動産ディベロッパーの過度なレバレッ
ジ追求による資金繰り悪化と、それに伴う住宅引き渡し遅延問題を引き起こしたことから、住宅販売
モデルの転換を加速させる。 
住宅都市農村建設部や中国人民銀行、国家金融監督管理総局などの関連省庁が一斉に通達や管理規
定を公布した。住宅販売制度に関する通達では、①予約販売の代金（購入者が支払う頭金と銀行から
振り込まれる住宅ローン）について住宅不動産プロジェクトごとの専用口座を通じた銀行側による資
金管理を強化すること、②新たに不動産用地や建設許可を取得するプロジェクトに対して「完成物件
での販売を優先的に選択」させ、建設許可を取得済みの新規プロジェクトについても完成物件での販
売を奨励すること、③住宅不動産プロジェクトごとに会社を設立し、メインバンクを選定して開発資
金の融資を受けること、などの方針を示した。一方、住宅ローンの新たな管理規定には、予約販売に
おける住宅ローンについて「厳格にプロジェクト竣工届出後に実行しなければならない」と明記した
ほか、これまで最長30年とされてきた住宅ローンの借入期間を40年に変更することを盛り込んだ。 
■ 「保交房」に一定のメド。ディベロッパーの法的整理が進む可能性も 
中国の不動産市場はこれまで、ディベロッパーが物件の竣工を待たずに販売を開始する予約販売方
式が一般的であり、住宅ローンも購入者が予約購入した時点で実行され、返済義務が生じる慣行とな
っていた。予約販売には、購入者にとって不動産価格の値上がり前に物件を確保できるメリットが、
ディベロッパーにとって不動産開発に投じた資金を早期に回収できるメリットがあった。 
しかし、財務レバレッジの追求による収益の最大化を狙うディベロッパーは、回収した予約販売代
金を新たな不動産開発用地の獲得や不動産とは関係のないビジネスへの投資に流用し、事業を急拡大
させた。その実態は自転車操業であり、市場の過熱を懸念した中国政府が2021年にディベロッパーへ
の資金調達規制 （いわゆる 「3つのレッドライン」 ） を正式導入するとたちまち資金繰りに行き詰まり、
社債の債務不履行（デフォルト）が頻発する。ディベロッパーの信用力が低下したところにゼロコロ
2026 年 9 月 30 日

### Page 2

2

ナ政策の強化が追い打ちをかける形で販売が急減し、ディベロッパーの資金繰りが一段と悪化したこ
とで建設工事の進捗が滞り、2022年に入って予約販売済み物件の引き渡し遅延が深刻化する。 
ここに至って予約販売のデメリットが明らかとなる。すなわち、物件引き渡しリスクを負担するの
は購入者であり、たとえ遅延しても住宅ローンの返済を続けなければならないことである。事態を重
く見た中国政府は、「保交楼（予約販売済み未完成物件の引き渡し保証。「保交房」も同じ）」のス
ローガンを掲げ、ディベロッパーの資金繰り支援に乗り出す。2024年1月には、当局が融資適格と判断
した不動産開発プロジェクトをまとめたホワイトリストを作成し、各銀行がこのリストに基づいてデ
ィベロッパーに資金を融通するスキームを構築して、問題の解消を急がせたのである。 
中国政府がこのタイミングで新規則を発表した背景には、この「保交房」に一定のメドが付いたと
判断したことがあるとみられる。事実、国務院の李強首相は2026年3月の全人代における政府活動報告
で「『保交房』の任務は全面的に完成した」と宣言している。また、見逃せない動きとして、広東省
広州市の中級人民法院（地方裁判所）が8月21日、実質的に経営破綻している某ディベロッパーについ
て、債権者による清算の申し立てを受理したことが挙げられる。 
デフォルトを起こした不動産ディベロッパーの多くはこれまで、倒産によって債務の返済や予約販
売物件の引き渡し、建設業者への代金支払いなどの義務・責任を放棄することが許されず、債権者か
ら債務の減免や返済猶予の合意を得つつゾンビ化した状態で経営を続けてきた。ディベロッパーの破
綻が相次げば、不良債権問題の深刻化によって金融システムが動揺するリスクを招くだけでなく、予
約販売物件の引き渡しが困難となって社会問題に発展したり、下請け業者の連鎖倒産で景気が急激に
冷え込む可能性もあったからである。 
今回の動きは、清算の申し立てを裁判所が「受理」しただけではあり、実際に清算が進むのかはな
お予断を許さないが、中国政府がディベロッパーの法的整理を前に進める意思を示したとみることも
できる。今後、清算手続きが順調に進むのか、他のディベロッパーについても同様の動きが広がるの
かを注意深く見守る必要があろう。 
■ 住宅販売モデル転換の社会的意義は大きいが、不動産市況への影響は軽微 
では、住宅販売モデルの転換は不動産市場にどのような影響を与えるであろうか。その前にまず、
不動産市場の現状を整理しておきたい。 
資金調達規制の強化をきっかけとした不動産不況は6年目に突入した。中国の不動産市場規模（販売
金額）は2021～25年にかけて半分に縮小し、販売面積や開発投資額は4割、新規着工面積に至っては7
割も減少した（図表1）。2026年も不況脱却の兆しは見えず、1～8月の販売面積は前年比▲12.1％、開
発投資額は同▲19.9％と低迷が続いている。開発投資の先行指標とされる不動産開発向け銀行貸出も
同▲33.3％となっており、ディベロッパーにとって資金繰りが厳しい状況は変わっていない。 
販売が大きく落ち込んだことで、市場には過剰な在庫が積み上がっている。みずほ総合研究所調査
部の試算によれば、建設中の物件を含む中国の住宅在庫（商用およびオフィス用を除く）は2025年末
時点で40.8億平米に達する（図表2）。2025年の住宅販売面積が7.3億平米であったことから、新規着工
がゼロになると仮定しても在庫をすべて消化するのに5年以上かかる計算である。中国政府は2024年5
月、地方政府による住宅在庫の買い取り策を発表し、同年10月には地方政府が専項債（特別債）の発
行で得た資金を買い取りに使うことも容認したが、在庫の消化は遅々として進んでいない。

### Page 3

3

住宅価格（主要70都市平均）は2021年7月のピーク時から新築で▲14％、中古で▲23％下落し、全体
として下落傾向に歯止めがかかっていない。ただ、図表3のとおり、底堅い需要のある大都市（一級都
市：北京、上海、広州、深圳）と膨大な在庫を抱える地方都市（二級都市：各省の省都。三級都市：
省都以下の主要都市）で二極化が顕著になっている。特に上海は堅調で、その新築住宅価格は2021年
7月から＋24％も上昇している。とはいえ、その堅調さは資産としての不動産保有の魅力を有する一部
の都市に限ったものであり、市況の回復が地方都市にまで広く波及する様子はうかがえない。 
住宅引き渡し遅延問題の発生以降、引き渡しリスクのない完成物件の購入が増加しつつあるが、予
約販売は2025年時点でも依然として住宅販売面積全体の6割超を占めている（図表4）。住宅販売モデ
ルの転換、すなわち予約販売から完成後販売への移行と予約販売における住宅ローン実行の後ろ倒し
は、住宅購入者の物件引き渡しリスクを大幅に軽減することになり、消費者保護の観点からもその社
会的意義は非常に大きいといえる。しかし、これに伴ってディベロッパーの資金繰りはより厳しいも
のとなる。ディベロッパーの資金調達に占める販売代金（頭金などの前受金と住宅ローン）の比率は
4割を超えているからである（図表5）。このため、短期的には新規の開発（土地の取得や新規着工）

4

が減少する可能性が高い。ただ、新規則は完成
後販売を全国一律に「強制」するのではなく、
あくまで「優先的に選択」させるとしているこ
とから、地方によってはディベロッパーの資金
繰りにも配慮した柔軟な対応がとられる可能
性もある。 
新規制が不動産市況に与える影響は、住宅ロ
ーンの最長借入期間の延長が新たな需要を掘
り起こすとしても、軽微であろう。不動産不況
を脱するためには、過剰在庫の消化とディベロ
ッパーの法的整理、不良債権の処理を着実に進
め、市場を健全な状態に戻すことが不可欠とい
えるが、それにはまだ相当の時間を要するもの
とみられる。

Document 060: 2026年9月经济数据点评：宏观政策调节力度加大，经济运行积极因素增多

Source PDF: all report pdf/2026年9月经济数据点评：宏观政策调节力度加大，经济运行积极因素增多.pdf
Report source: 诚通证券
Date: 2026-10-20
Pages: 11
Status: ok

### Page 1

宏观经济
宏观政策调节力度加大，经济运行积极因素增多
——2026 年 9 月经济数据点评 宏观点评
8 月以来，宏观政策调节力度加大，叠加出口保持高增长，经济运行积
极因素增多。
实际 GDP 增速回升，GDP 平减指数持续正增长。存量政策加快落地，
加上出口保持高增长，三季度实际 GDP 同比增速从 4.3%上升至 4.5%。三季
度名义 GDP 同比增长 6%，GDP 平减指数连续 2 个季度同比正增长。AI 产
业链需求旺盛，加之能源供给扰动，物价有所改善。三季度，CPI 同比上涨
0.73%，涨幅较二季度下降 0.4 个百分点；PPI 同比上涨 4.1%，涨幅较二季
度上升 0.5 个百分点；GDP 平减指数同比增长 1.5%，增幅和二季度基本持
平，连续 2 个季度正增长。
出口高增长加之内需修复，工业生产加速扩张。9 月，工业生产同比增
速 5.5%，较 8 月上升 0.3 个百分点。其原因有三方面。一是高温多雨天气干
扰消退，加之季末效应拉动。二是出口同比增长 36.3%，增速较上月上升 11.3

个百分点，有效支撑工业生产。三是政府债券发行、新型政策性金融工具、
“六张网”等存量政策加快落地，带动相关制造业生产扩张。分行业看，装
备制造业和高技术制造业生产扩张较快。年内，全球信用脉冲仍处在向上阶
段，全球制造业或将保持较高景气度，同时 AI 基建尚未完成，中国出口或
仍保持韧性。加之存量政策加快落地，我们预计工业生产将基本保持稳定。
存量政策效果显现，当月投资同比降幅收窄。9 月，固定资产投资累计
同比下降 7.2%，降幅停止扩大；当月同比降幅从 11%收窄至 7.2%，主要是
因为当月基建投资、制造业投资同比降幅收窄。“六张网”建设加快落实；
政府债券加快发行及使用；新型政策性金融工具实施；下调抵押补充贷款
（PSL）利率 0.25 个百分点，并扩大 PSL 支持领域，另增加多项再贷款额度。
受此影响，基建投资当月同比降幅从 6.9%收窄至 5.4%；制造业投资当月同
比降幅从 6.1%收窄至 1%。
受中秋错位等因素影响，消费增速小幅回升。9 月，社会消费品零售总
额同比增速从 0.4%上升至 1.1%；季调环比增速从-0.16%上升至 0.35%。消

费增速回升主要受以旧换新、中秋错位以及居民收入增速上升的影响。三季
度居民人均可支配收入累计同比增长 5.3%，增速较上季度上升 0.1 个百分点，
连续 2 个季度改善。商品零售总额同比增长 1%，增速上升 0.7 个百分点。
餐饮消费同比增长 2.1%，增速上升 1 个百分点。
信用创造有所改善，保障经济平稳运行。9 月，存量、增量政策加快落
地，政府债券加快发行，信用创造有所修复，新增社融 3.55 万亿元，同比多
增 177 亿元。其中，政府债券净融资 1.5 万亿元，同比多增 3469 亿元。信用
脉冲（12 个月新增社融同比增速）从-10.7%小幅上升至-10.1%。四季度，政
府债券融资或继续同比多增，信用脉冲可能继续回升，社融、M2 同比增速
有望阶段性改善。
 风险提示：新增政策存在不确定性；房地产市场止跌回稳难度超预期；
物价改善内生动力不足；海外需求出现拐点，出口增速下滑；美联储加息路
径不确定性较大。
邢曙光（分析师）
xingshuguang@cctgsc.com.cn

### Page 3

8 月以来，宏观政策调节力度加大，叠加出口保持高增长，经济运行积极因素
增多。三季度，实际 GDP 同比增速从 4.3%上升至 4.5%；物价改善，名义 GDP 同
比增长 6%，GDP 平减指数连续 2 个季度同比正增长。9 月，工业生产同比增速上
升 0.3 个百分点至 5.5%；服务业生产指数同比增速上升 0.7 个百分点至 4.8%；固定
资产投资当月同比降幅从 11%收窄至 7.2%；社会消费品零售总额同比增速从 0.4%
上升至 1.1%；新增社融 3.55 万亿元，同比多增 177 亿元。四季度，政府债券融资
或继续同比多增，信用创造有望持续改善，保障经济平稳运行。
1、 实际 GDP 增速回升，GDP 平减指数持续正增长
存量政策加快落地，加上出口保持高增长，三季度实际 GDP 同比增速从 4.3%
上升至 4.5%。其中，第一产业增加值实际同比增速 3.6%，较二季度下降 0.1 个百
分点；第二产业增加值实际同比增速 3.4%，较二季度上升 0.4 个百分点；第三产业

增加值实际同比增速 5.2%，较二季度上升 0.1 个百分点。
三季度名义 GDP 同比增长 6%，高于实际 GDP 增速，GDP 平减指数连续 2
个季度同比正增长。AI 产业链需求旺盛，加之能源供给扰动，物价有所改善。三
季度，CPI 同比上涨 0.73%，涨幅较二季度下降 0.4 个百分点；PPI 同比上涨 4.1%，
涨幅较二季度上升 0.5 个百分点；GDP 平减指数同比增长 1.5%，增幅和二季度基
本持平，连续 2 个季度正增长。
图 1： 三季度实际 GDP 同比增长 4.5% 图 2： GDP 平减指数连续 2 个季度同比正增长
资料来源：Wind，诚通证券研究所 资料来源：Wind，诚通证券研究所
2、 出口高增长加之内需修复，工业生产加速扩张
9 月，工业生产同比增速 5.5%，较 8 月上升 0.3 个百分点。其原因有三方面。
一是高温多雨天气干扰消退，加之季末效应拉动。二是出口同比增长 36.3%，增速
较上月上升 11.3 个百分点，有效支撑工业生产。三是政府债券发行、新型政策性

金融工具、“六张网”等存量政策加快落地，带动相关制造业生产扩张。

### Page 4

图 3： 工业、服务业生产增速回升 图 4： 出口拉动工业生产
资料来源：Wind，诚通证券研究所 资料来源：Wind，诚通证券研究所
其中，出口高增长主要有两方面原因。一是全球制造业景气度仍比较高。9 月
全球制造业 PMI 从 52.3%上升至 53%，连续 14 个月维持在 50%以上。美国 ISM 制
造业 PMI 保持在 54.5%的较高水平，欧元区 Markit 制造业 PMI 从 52.7%上升至 52.9%，
日本 Markit 制造业 PMI 54.1%。中国对主要经济体出口保持较高增速，对美国、欧
盟、东盟、日本、韩国、拉美出口同比增速分别从 34.4%、6.6%、30.2%、8.2%、
49.3%、17.5%上升至 39.6%、25.3%、52.3%、15.8%、68.2%、24.7%。劳动密集型
产品出口同比增速从 9.2%上升至 22.8%。二是 AI 产业链需求旺盛。与 AI 产业链
相关的高新技术产品、机电产品出口同比增速较高，分别为 63.7%、43.7%。其中，

集成电路出口同比增速更是高达 130.6%。
图 5： 全球制造业景气度仍较高 图 6： 各类产品出口增速上升
资料来源：Wind，诚通证券研究所 资料来源：Wind，诚通证券研究所
分行业看，装备制造业和高技术制造业生产扩张较快。计算机、通信和其他电
子设备制造业，专用设备制造业，铁路、船舶、航空航天和其他运输设备制造业，
石油和天然气开采业，通用设备制造业，金属制品业，电气机械及器材制造业，汽
车制造业生产同比增速较高，分别为 16.4%、12.1%、11.7%、9.8%、8.5%、8.2%、
8.1%、6.7%。此外，受供给端约束及产能治理影响，有色金属冶炼及压延加工业生
产同比下降 5.1%。受煤矿停产整顿影响，煤炭开采和洗选业生产同比下降 3.3%。
受房地产投资、基建投资低迷拖累，非金属矿物制品业生产同比下降 2.4%。

### Page 5

年内，全球信用脉冲仍处在向上阶段，全球制造业或将保持较高景气度，同时
AI 基建尚未完成，中国出口或仍保持韧性。加之存量政策加快落地，我们预计工
业生产将基本保持稳定。
图 7： 装备制造业和高技术制造业生产扩张较快
资料来源：Wind，诚通证券研究所
3、 存量政策效果显现，当月投资同比降幅收窄
9 月，固定资产投资累计同比下降 7.2%，降幅停止扩大；当月同比降幅从 11%
收窄至 7.2%，主要是因为当月基建投资、制造业投资同比降幅收窄。
存量政策效果显现，基建投资、制造业投资降幅收窄。8 月以来，存量政策加
快落地。“六张网”建设加快落实；政府债券加快发行及使用，9 月政府债券净融
资 1.5 万亿元，同比多增 3469 亿元；新型政策性金融工具实施，资金规模达 8000
亿元，较上年增加 3000 亿元；下调抵押补充贷款（PSL）利率 0.25 个百分点，并
扩大 PSL 支持领域，另增加多项再贷款额度。受此影响，基建投资当月同比降幅
从 6.9%收窄至 5.4%；制造业投资当月同比降幅从 6.1%收窄至 1%。设备工器具购

置投资累计同比增速从 9.3%上升至 9.7%。铁路、船舶、航空航天和其他运输设备
制造业，计算机、通信和其他电子设备制造业，电气机械及器材制造业，通用设备
制造业投资分别累计同比增长 15.8%、9.6%、1.2%、1.1%。

### Page 6

图 8： 当月投资同比降幅收窄 图 9： 当月基建、制造业投资同比降幅收窄
资料来源：Wind，诚通证券研究所 资料来源：Wind，诚通证券研究所
图 10： 工业产能利用率仍处于低位 图 11： 设备工器具购置投资增速较高
资料来源：Wind，诚通证券研究所 资料来源：Wind，诚通证券研究所
房地产市场止跌回稳难度较大。虽然房地产市场需求结构性改善，但整体商品
房销售面积累计同比下降 13.2%，降幅扩大 1.1 个百分点。整体库存压力较大，房
企资金压力仍存，房地产投资累计同比下降 20.6%，降幅扩大 0.7 个百分点。与此
同时，房屋新开工面积累计同比下降 24.9%，降幅扩大 0.1 个百分点；房屋施工面
积累计同比下降 12.8%，降幅仍较大。
房价分化。一线城市房地产价格有所改善，但持续性仍待观察。一线城市二手
住宅销售价格环比上涨 0.2%，涨幅扩大 0.1 个百分点，连续 7 个月上涨。二线城市
二手住宅销售价格环比下降 0.2%，降幅收窄 0.1 个百分点。三线城市二手住宅销售

价格环比下降 0.3%，降幅持平上月。

### Page 7

图 12： 房地产投资降幅扩大 图 13： 房价分化
资料来源：Wind，诚通证券研究所 资料来源：Wind，诚通证券研究所
4、 受中秋错位等因素影响，消费增速小幅回升
9 月，社会消费品零售总额同比增速从 0.4%上升至 1.1%；季调环比增速从
-0.16%上升至 0.35%。消费增速回升主要受以旧换新、中秋错位以及居民收入增速
上升的影响。三季度居民人均可支配收入累计同比增长 5.3%，增速较上季度上升
0.1 个百分点，连续 2 个季度改善。
商品零售总额同比增长 1%，增速上升 0.7 个百分点。通讯器材消费同比增长
31.1%，文化办公用品消费同比增长 7.9%，主要是因为以旧换新政策优化实施，加
上新一批补贴资金下发，以及新学期开学需求释放。受中秋错位影响，粮油、食品，
饮料，烟酒消费同比增速分别上升 2.7、0.1、1.3 个百分点，至 6.7%、5%、13.8%。
受油价上涨影响，石油及制品消费同比增速上升 6.2 个百分点至-0.8%。受换季影响，
服装鞋帽针纺织品消费同比增速上升 1.7 个百分点至 1.2%。此外，汽车消费补贴退

坡加之高基数，汽车消费同比下降 21.2%。受房地产市场拖累，家具、建筑及装潢
材料消费分别同比下降 3%、9.2%。
与此同时，受中秋错位影响，服务消费增速有所回升。餐饮消费当月同比增长
2.1%，增速上升 1 个百分点。服务零售额累计同比增长 5%，增速上升 0.1 个百分
点。
服务业生产指数同比增速上升 0.7 个百分点至 4.8%。其中，信息传输、软件和
信息技术服务业，租赁和商务服务业，交通运输、仓储和邮政业生产同比增速分别
上升 0.2、0.5、0.6 个百分点至 9.8%、9.5%、5.4%。

### Page 8

图 14： 居民收入增速上升，消费边际改善

图 15： 通讯器材等消费增速较高

5、 信用创造有所改善，保障经济平稳运行
9 月，存量、增量政策加快落地，政府债券加快发行，信用创造有所修复，新
增社融 3.55 万亿元，同比多增 177 亿元。其中，政府债券净融资 1.5 万亿元，同比
多增 3469 亿元。与此同时，民间融资、信贷仍待进一步改善。新增贷款 1.08 万亿
元，同比少增 2100 亿元。其中，新增居民中长期贷款 874 亿元，同比少增 1626 亿
元；新增企业中长期贷款 8900 亿元，同比少增 200 亿元。
虽然社融余额、M2 同比增速分别从 7.2%、7.5%继续下降至 7.1%、7.2%，不
过领先指标信用脉冲（12 个月新增社融同比增速）从-10.7%小幅上升至-10.1%。四
季度，政府债券融资或继续同比多增，信用脉冲可能继续回升，社融、M2 同比增
速有望阶段性改善。但由于全年广义赤字规模基本持平上年、广义赤字率可能下降，

### Page 9

信用脉冲、社融增速、M2 增速、广义财政支出增速回升幅度仍需再观察。

投资评级说明
诚通证券行业评级体系：推荐、中性、回避
推荐： 未来6－12个月，预计该行业指数表现强于同期市场基准指数。
中性： 未来6－12个月，预计该行业指数表现基本与同期市场基准指数持平。
回避： 未来6－12个月，预计该行业指数表现弱于同期市场基准指数。

市场基准指数为沪深 300 指数。
诚通证券公司评级体系：强烈推荐、推荐、中性、回避
强烈推荐：未来 6－12 个月，预计该公司股价相对同期市场基准指数涨幅在 20%以上。该评级由分析师给出。
推荐：未来 6－12 个月，预计该公司股价相对同期市场基准指数涨幅介于 5%－20%。该评级由分析师给出。
中性：未来 6－12 个月，预计该公司股价相对同期市场基准指数变动幅度介于-5%－5%。该评级由分析师给出。
回避：未来 6－12 个月，预计该公司股价相对同期市场基准指数跌幅在 5%以上。该评级由分析师给出。
市场基准指数为沪深 300 指数。

OSAT 展望樂觀，SOIC 機台具技術優勢 
 2027 主要成長動能仍為 OSAT:2026 年弘塑產能擴充一倍 ，1Q26 已
經完成擴建 ，3Q26 新產能已經滿載。 外包部分 ， 目前外包比例33%，
未來佔比會下降，目前外包月產能一個月 5-6 台，因為外包產能受
限，同業也在搶外包產能。2027 主要成長動能仍為 OSAT，今年晶
圓代工客戶營收高於 OSAT，2027 年 OSAT 營收超過晶圓代工客戶，
SOIC 機台去年小量，今年量增，2027 跟 2026 差不多略增，2028
才會明顯大增，主要還是跟隨晶圓代工客戶 SOIC 擴廠進度。 
 2028-2029 年 PLP 機台出貨量會很明顯放大:PLP 及 CoPoS 設備機
台單價很高，初估有機會是 CoWoS 翻倍，PLP 進度比較快，PLP
規格弘塑 310*310、510*515、600*600 都有，再大到 700 以上就不
做了，晶圓代工客戶規格是 310*310 但目前進度比較慢，還在實驗
線階段。力成 510*515 進度最快，4Q26 會拉第一批機台，2027 隨
力成購置的友達廠進度持續拉機台，2028-2029 年 PLP 機台出貨量
會很明顯放大。 
 維持買進評等，目標價 3000 元：基本面無變化，預估數字也沒做調
整，故維持買進評等 ，預估 2026 年 EPS 64.81 元，2027 年 EPS 93.97
元，目標價 3000 元。(2027 PER 30x)

弘塑為本土封測設備龍頭廠商 
 弘塑為本土封測設備龍頭廠商 
弘塑為本土封測設備龍頭廠商，主要利基產品為半導體後段濕製程設備，並延
伸相關化學品、檢測設備代理及軟體解決方案。1H26 產品結構可區分為機台檢
測產品(65%)、化學產品(20%)、代理設備(15%)。2013 年合併化學品公司添鴻
(CLC)、2018 年底合併通路商佳霖(ClC)、併購資訊系統廠太引(Tyne)，投資美
國 X-Ray 檢測公司 SVXR。 客戶為國內半導體 、 光電 、記憶體及面板產業大廠，
包括台積電、矽品、日月光、力成、星科金朋、精材、聯電、茂矽、采鈺、穏
懋、中芯、宏達等。

2Q26 毛 利率 39.37%， 低於預
期，2Q26 EPS 11.18 元 
 2Q26 毛利率 39.37%，低於預期，2Q26 EPS 11.18 元 
弘塑 2Q26 EPS 11.18 元，低於元富預估的 12.34 元，獲利達成率 91%。本業
來看，2Q26 營收 17.27 億元，+8%QOQ，+6%YOY，2Q26 毛利率 39.37%，
較 1Q26 回升 5.1 個百分點，然仍低於預估的 41%，主要是外包成本及急單增
加影響部分毛利率，預期 3Q26 毛利率持續向上改善，2Q26 EPS 11.18 元。

弘塑 3Q26 營收 22.67 億元，+31.3%QOQ，+51.8%YOY，營收達成率 98%，
略低於預期，預估 3Q26 毛利率 41%，預估 3Q26 EPS 16.47 元。

營運逐季成長，OSA T展望樂觀

營運逐季成長，OSA T展望樂觀 
2026 年弘塑擴產一倍 ，1Q26 已經完成擴建 ，3Q26 新產能已經滿載。 外包部分 ，
目前外包比例 33%，未來佔比會下降，目前外包月產能一個月 5-6 台，因為外
包產能受限 ， 同業也在搶外包產能 。2027 年擴產再 50-100%， 以租用廠房為主。
2027 主要成長動能還是 OSAT，今年晶圓代工客戶營收高於 OSAT，2027 年
OSAT 營收超過晶圓代工客戶，SOIC 機台去年小量，今年量增，2027 跟 2026
差不多略增 ，2028 才會明顯大增 ， 主要還是跟隨晶圓代工客戶 SOIC 擴廠進度，
SOIC 機台種類 6-7 種。

PLP 及 CoPoS 設備機台單價很高，是 CoWoS 翻倍，PLP 進度比較快，PLP
規格弘塑 310*310、510*515、600*600 都有，再大到 700 以上就不做了，晶圓

續拉機台，2028-2029 年 PLP 量會很明顯放大。

CPO 部分，確定會出貨的是 EIC 跟 PIC 兩個晶片 Bonding 在一起的設備，跟
SOIC 類似，PIC 的機台目前進度很慢，因為 35 族會有污染問題，Sillicon 平面
長線做濕蝕刻這塊不做，會產生治癌物。

維持買進，目標價 3000 元

維持買進評等，目標價 3000 元 
基本面無變化，預估數字也沒做調整，OSAT 拉貨樂觀，長線 SOIC 機台放量可

標價 3000 元。(2027 PER 30x)

風險提示 
出貨不如預期，ASP 不如預期。

營收走勢圖

研究員/張均壕 JUN_HAO1@uni-psg.com

研究部對拓凱未來營運看法轉趨正向 ，主要是考量：(1)占營收比
重最大的自行車業務在 2H26 出現改善，隨著主力客戶新品銷售表
現較佳，帶動訂單增加，預期 2027 年自行車業務有望止跌回溫。
(2)航太、安全帽及其他類業務接單保持強勁，其中航太受惠於航
空座椅需求增加，安全帽則有高階產品及新客戶訂單貢獻，加上精
密塑件業務持續擴張 ， 將有助於優化產品組合 。(3) 2Q26 受子公司
一次性費用及匯率等因素干擾，毛利率與獲利下滑，但隨下半年出
貨規模擴大、產品組合改善，預期 2H26 獲利能力將逐步回升。研
究部預估拓凱 2026 年 EPS 13.52 元(-9%YOY)，2027 年EPS 15.28
元(+13%YOY)。因公司營運重回成長軌道，且產品組合改善有利於
中長期獲利表現，故以 2027 年獲利為評價基礎，給予 12 倍本益
比，目標價 183 元，投資評等由中立調升至買進。

重點摘要：

1. 主力客戶新車種上市，自行車業務回溫 ： 
拓凱營收約有 50% 來自碳纖維自行車車架與組件，最大核心
客戶為全球知名自行車 品牌-Specialized。雖然公司的自行
車業務在1H26衰退近二成，但受惠主力客戶 年中新品上市並
在終端市場銷售亮眼 ， 客戶對拓凱積極追加車架 、 輪圈與前叉
等複材組件訂單 ， 帶動拓凱近期自行車業務顯著改善 。 在去年
下半年低基期與今年新車拉貨潮帶動下，預計 2026全年自行
車營收減幅將收斂至個位數（研究部估減少8%YoY）。2027年
隨客戶新品需求與庫存去化完畢，預 估自行車業務營收將能
回溫，成長3%YoY。

2. 安全帽、航太及其他類業務展望正向，成長動能強勁： 
自行車以外業務為拓凱營運成長的核心動能 。 其中 ， 拓凱的安
全帽業務近年出貨增加 ， 且產品結構大幅改善 ， 高階產品占比
已達2/3，預計帶動今年安全帽營收大幅增長30%YoY，且因引
進電子智能安全帽新客戶，預期明年該業務營收仍可成長
7%YoY。球拍業務方面，今年出貨暢旺，預期今年球拍營收能
創下歷史新高 ， 全年度球拍營收預計成長18%YoY。 不過 ， 因近
兩年基期拉高，明年看法較謹慎，預計小幅衰退5%YoY。航太
醫療業務方面 ， 航太接單展望樂觀 ， 預期今年該類別營收增長
14%YoY，且1Q27起新增飛機座椅訂單將正式投產 ， 預計明年該
類別營收將再有12%YoY的增速 。 另外 ， 歸類在其他類之子公司

### Page 2

請參見末頁重要批露及免責聲明  2

新鴻洲部分，受惠自行車煞把手、電子TOF精密塑件等產品出
貨增加 ， 營運動能依舊偏強 ， 預計該業務2026年、2027年營收
分別成長23%、13%YoY。

3. 一次性負面因素淡化，3Q26毛利率與獲利將回升： 
回顧拓凱2Q26財報 ， 受提列子公司一次性費用的衝擊 ， 導致單
季毛利率下滑至31.3%(季減3個百分點，年減6.7個百分點)，
加上業外有逾6千萬元的匯損，拖累季度獲利表現，2Q26稅後
淨利2.67億元(-19%QoQ，-5%YoY)，2Q26EPS下滑至2.94元。然
而 ， 此費用屬於非經常性的短期會計調整 ， 本業高階複材競爭
力並未受損 。 下半年隨自行車業務改善 ， 拓凱3Q26合併營收回
升至27.16億元(+6%QoQ，+12%YoY)。 此外 ， 由於一次性因素淡
化 ， 加上高階安全帽 、 航太及其他類產品出貨增加 ， 產品組合
優化，研究部預估拓凱3Q26毛利率將回升至33.8%，營業利益
5.41億元(+37%QoQ，+13%YoY)，稅後淨利3.55億元(+33%QoQ，
+1%YoY)，3Q26EPS 3.91元，獲利將較第2季明顯改善。

4. 2026年營收近百億元，預估2026年EPS 13.52元： 
受惠於安全帽 、 球拍 、 航太及其他業務成長 ， 抵銷自行車業務
的衰退 ， 預期拓凱全年營收可望接近百億元 ， 研究部預估拓凱
2026年合併營收99.73億元(+8%YoY)。不過，由於2Q26受一次
性費用及匯率等因素干擾，今年度毛利率預估降至33.1%，營
業利益16.91億元(-2%YoY)，稅後淨利12.28億元(-9%YoY)，
EPS 13.52元。

5. 各業務成長帶動獲利回升，預估2027年EPS 15.28元： 
展望2027年 ， 隨自行車業務恢復成長 ， 航太及安全帽新訂單持
續貢獻，以及其他類業務穩健擴張，研究部預估 2027年營收
104.34億元(+5%YoY)， 毛利率回升至33.8%， 營業利益18.50億
元(+9%YoY)，稅後淨利13.88億元(+13%YoY)，EPS 15.28元。

6. 越南產能持續擴充，並積極佈局新應用商機 ： 
因應客戶分散生產基地與供應鏈調整需求，拓凱持續擴充越
南複合材料產品線 ， 目前除自行車前叉 、 車架等零組件外 ， 亦
逐步增加航空座椅零組件的生產，未來航太板材及球拍等產
品亦有機會導入越南製造 。 公司也已著手規劃越南二期廠房 ，
強化海外生產彈性 。 此外 ， 中科后里新廠除作為營運總部及研
發據點外 ， 也將支援新業務開發 。 初期鎖定人形機器人關鍵的
腰部與膝蓋複材結構件與客戶進行共同開發，同時也積極切
入農業與商用無人機 、 低軌衛星等先端應用 。 子公司新鴻洲方

面 ， 則運用精密射出及模具技術 ， 計畫開拓半導體與人形機器
人零件等應用 。 雖然新事業短期營收貢獻仍有限 ， 但隨產品開
發與客戶認證推進，長期有助於拓展複合材料及精密零組件
的應用領域。

### Page 1

현대차 (005380)

EARNINGS PREVIEW Company Analysis | 자동차/로봇 | 2026.10.16  
4분기 반등 기대

3Q26 Preview 
현대차는 3Q26E 매출액 42.4조원(-9.4% YoY, -13.9% QoQ), 영업이익 2.5조원(-
2.8% YoY, -13.5% QoQ, OPM 5.8%)을 기록, 컨센서스를 하회할 전망이다. 각 부문
별 영업이익은 자동차 1.7조원(-2.0% YoY, -14.9% QoQ, OPM 4.8%), 금융 5,501억
원(-4.5% YoY, -27.5% QoQ, OPM 6.9%)으로 예상한다. 
생산 정상화 및 신차 확대를 통한 수익성 개선 기대 
현대차는 글로벌 도매 판매 91.8만대(-11.5% 이하 YoY), 중국 제외 판매량은 90.9
만대(-9.6%)를 기록했다. 7~8월에 발생한 파업 영향으로 약 5.5만대 규모의 생산 차
질이 발생했다. 파업 영향으로 HEV 판매 증가율이 1%에 그치면서, HEV 판매 확대에 
따른 믹스 개선 효과도 시장 기대에 미치지 못했다.  
4분기부터는 3분기 파업 영향으로 지연된 생산량 회복 및 신차 확대가 본격화되면서, 
전분기 대비 매출 및 수익성 개선이 기대된다. 동사는 보급형 전기차 아이오닉3의 튀
르키예 생산을 9월부터 시작했으며, 이를 통해 유럽 시장 판매량은 점차 회복될 전망
이다. 또한, 1) HMGMA를 통한 HEV 미국 현지 생산 확대, 2) 제네시스 HEV 모델 순
차적 출시를 통해 내년부터 북미를 중심으로 믹스 개선이 본격화될 전망이다. 
투자의견은 Buy를 유지하고 목표주가는 520,000원으로 하향 
2027년 영업이익은 11.2조원(+10.2%)를 기록할 것으로 예상된다. 원화 약세 지속을 
반영해 이익 추정치를 하향하며, 이에 따라 목표주가를 하향한다. 다만, 현재 주가는 
12M Fwd P/E 기준 6.1배로 Legacy OEM 평균(7.7배)보다 낮은 수준이다. 1) RMAC 
성과 구체화, 2) RA(로봇 생산법인) 착공 등 Physical AI 사업이 구체화될 경우 밸류에
이션 할증이 가능할 전망이다. 
 Analyst 이병근 
bglee@ls-sec.co.kr

Document 253: Chartpack _ Advanced economies_ Resilience to shock after shock (2026-10-06)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Chartpack _ Advanced economies_ Resilience to shock after shock (2026-10-06).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-10-06
Pages: 12
Status: ok

### Page 1

By Advanced Economies Team
Advanced Economies:
Resilience to shock after shock
October 2026

### Page 2

• The global economy is in the midst of a growth cycle. As a result, GDP growth is more resilient to shocks than initially
anticipated.
• The figures for the second quarter bear this out, despite the sharp rise in oil prices, whose negative impact on growth is offset
by a series of reflationary forces.
• The driving force behind the current growth cycle is clear: artificial intelligence (AI).
• The world’s major economies are currently engaged in a broader push to expand their capital goods investment.

• While European and US households are experiencing more or less the same energy price shock, inflation and household
consumption remain higher in the US.
• Higher and above-target inflation calls for monetary tightening.
• Inflation is exceeding the target set by three of the four central banks examined in this publication (the US, the UK, the
Eurozone). Inflation is currently expected to peak in Q4 2026 or Q1 2027, but there is a significant risk that energy inflation will

spread further to other components of inflation in the future.
• Inflation can also be a sign of (too much) economic strength (in the US).
• Monetary policies also have to offset the inflationary effects of fiscal policy (particularly in Japan).
• The outbreak of the conflict in Iran acted as a catalyst: the ensuing rise in energy prices contributes to the recent increase in
long-term rates (which had been trending downwards at the start of the year) to levels close to those of 2002.

• The other issue is the high level of fiscal deficits (too high for a period of growth), reflecting different fiscal trajectories. In the
US, the UK, France and Germany the public debt-to-GDP ratio is increasing, while it has stabilized in Italy and decreased in
Spain and Japan.
• The rise in long-term interest rates will weigh on public finances. The fiscal arithmetic is simpler today than it will be in 2030.
By 2030, the effective rate will have risen and caught up with GDP growth.

For more information, please refer to our publication:
EcoPerspectives – Advanced Economies | 3rd quarter of 2026, as of September 24, 2026– Economic Research– BNP Paribas
Advanced economies: Resilience to shock after shock

Document 266: CTCP Nhựa Bình Minh - Báo cáo cập nhật 2Q2026 (2026-09-23)

Source PDF: all report pdf/CTCP Nhựa Bình Minh - Báo cáo cập nhật 2Q2026 (2026-09-23).pdf

### Page 1

Doanh thu 2Q2026 tăng trưởng chậm 
nhưng biên lợi nhuận gộp vẫn được 
duy trì do tăng giá bán

Sản lượng tiêu thụ 6M2026 chững lại 
do tâm lý nhập hàng thận trọng và 
hoạt động xây dựng phục hồi chậm

Biên lợi nhuận gộp được dự báo sẽ 
thu hẹp cuối năm, phản ánh chi phí 
nguyên liệu cao hơn trong 1Q2026

Khuyến nghị TRUNG LẬP, giá mục 
tiêu 166,100 VND/cổ phiếu 
 Doanh thu/LNST 2Q2026 đạt lần lượt 1,320/367 tỷ VND (+1%/+11% YoY). Lũy kế 
6M2026, doanh thu đạt 2,777 tỷ VND (+3% YoY) trong khi LNST ước đạt 671 tỷ VND 
(+9% YoY). Biên lãi gộp 6M2026 cải thiện đạt 47.0% (+240 bps YoY), chủ yếu do việc 
tăng giá bán giúp doanh nghiệp bù đắp được mức tăng của chi phí đầu vào.

Lũy kế 6M2026, sản lượng tiêu thụ của BMP ước đạt 43,920 tấn, giảm 5% svck. Việc 
BMP thu hẹp các chương trình chiết khấu, với mức chiết khấu bình quân khoảng 
5.2% trong 2Q2026, làm giảm động lực nhập hàng của các đại lý và khiến sản lượng 
tiêu thụ chậm lại. Dù vậy, thị phần toàn quốc của BMP ước đạt 28% (+1 đpt svck) 
nhờ các doanh nghiệp khác trong ngành gặp khó khăn khi giá hạt nhựa tăng mạnh.

Biên lợi nhuận gộp của BMP dự kiến thu hẹp trong 2H2026 trước áp lực chi phí 
nguyên liệu đầu vào tăng. Tuy nhiên, chúng tôi kỳ vọng mức độ ảnh hưởng sẽ thấp 
hơn so với các doanh nghiệp trong ngành do (1) các lô nguyên liệu giá cao từ 
1Q2026 đã được đưa vào sản xuất và tiêu thụ một phần trong 1H2026; (2) BMP chủ 
động nâng gấp đôi hàng tồn kho để ứng phó với biến động bất lợi của giá nguyên 
liệu; (3) hơn 90% nhu cầu nguyên liệu uPVC được cung ứng từ các đối tác trong 
nước như hệ sinh thái SCG tại Việt Nam, AGC Chemicals và LSP; và (4) công ty duy 
trì giá bán bình quân ở mức cao.

Dựa trên kết quả kinh doanh và định giá, chúng tôi khuyến nghị TRUNG LẬP với cổ 
phiếu BMP, giá mục tiêu là 166,100 VND/cổ phiếu (tương ứng với mức tăng 14.3% 
so với giá đóng cửa ngày 23/09/2026).

Tăng trưởng doanh thu và lợi nhuận thuần 5 năm gần nhất Hoạt động kinh doanh

Nhựa Bình Minh (BMP) là doanh nghiệp hàng đầu 
trong lĩnh vực sản xuất ống nhựa với thị phần 
dẫn đầu tại thị trường nội địa (27% vào cuối 
2023), thị trường chính tập trung tại khu vực 
miền Nam. BMP có 4 nhà máy với tổng công suất 
đạt 150,000 tấn/năm. 
Nguồn: BMP, KBSV

Điểm nhấn đầu tư  Chú thích 
Kỳ vọng đà phục hồi của xây dựng dân dụng và bất động sản miền Nam sẽ thúc đẩy sản lượng tiêu 
thụ tăng trưởng trong 2026–2027. 
 Chi tiết ở phần dưới của báo cáo 
Giá PVC được kỳ vọng sẽ hạ nhiệt và khả năng giữ giá bán đầu ra ở mức cao giúp BMP giữ biên lợi 
nhuận gộp ở nền cao. 
 Chi tiết ở phần dưới của báo cáo

### Page 4

4

Nhu cầu nhập hàng thận trọng và 
cắt giảm các đợt chiết khấu trong 
kỳ của BMP làm giảm sản lượng tiêu 
thụ trong 2Q2026

Sự hồi phục của nhu cầu xây dựng 
dân dụng và nguồn cung các dự án 
bất động sản tại miền Nam sẽ thúc 
đẩy tăng trưởng sản lượng 2H2026 
 Sản lượng tiêu thụ trong 2Q2026 ước đạt 20,100 tấn, giảm 11% svck, nâng tổng 
sản lượng tiêu thụ 6M2026 ước đạt 43,920 tấn, giảm 5% svck do các yếu tố bất 
lợi từ bên ngoài như (1) giá hạt nhựa tăng cao khiến thị trường dè chừng trong 
việc nhập hàng vì lo ngại nguồn cung bất ổn (2) BMP đã thu hẹp các chương 
trình chiết khấu so với 2025 ngay trước thời điểm xung đột bùng nổ, giúp tỷ lệ 
chi phí bán hàng/doanh thu về mức 11.3%, giảm 800 bps so với 1Q2026 ở mức 
19.3%, nhưng đồng thời cũng khiến sản lượng tiêu thụ của BMP suy giảm do 
chính sách bán hàng thắt chặt hơn với các đối thủ có thị phần thấp hơn.

Chúng tôi kỳ vọng sản lượng tiêu thụ của BMP dần hồi phục từ 3Q2026 khi nhu 
cầu nhập hàng của các đại lý cải thiện sau giai đoạn thận trọng trong nửa đầu 
năm. Về cuối năm, hoạt động xây dựng dự kiến tăng tốc khi điều kiện thi công 
thuận lợi hơn sau mùa mưa. Bên cạnh đó, việc triển khai các dự án nhà ở tại 
TP.HCM và các tỉnh lân cận được kỳ vọng hỗ trợ nhu cầu ống nhựa trong giai 
đoạn tới. Theo đó, chúng tôi dự phóng sản lượng tiêu thụ của BMP lần lượt đạt 
96,695/100,125/96,750 tấn trong 2026/2027/2028.

Biểu đồ 3. Tương quan giữa tỷ lệ chi phí bán hàng/doanh 
thu và lợi nhuận thuần/sản lượng tiêu thụ  
 Biểu đồ 4. Dự phóng nguồn cung bất động sản tại Hà Nội và Hồ 
Chí Minh

Nguồn: BMP, KBSV  Nguồn: CBRE, KBSV

Biểu đồ 5. Vòng quay hàng tồn kho của BMP so với các doanh nghiệp trong ngành 
   BMP duy trì vòng quay dẫn đầu trong 
ngành nhờ lợi thế nguồn nguyên liệu và thị 
phần đứng đầu tại miền Nam. Tuy nhiên, 
xu hướng suy giảm các quý gần đây về 
mức 5.3 lần, giảm 0.21 điểm QoQ do nhu 
cầu tiêu thụ toàn ngành suy giảm.

### Page 5

5

Lợi thế từ hệ sinh thái công ty mẹ 
SCG và đối tác Nhật Bản giúp BMP 
có nhiều dư địa để nâng cao hiệu 
quả hoạt động

Theo chia sẻ từ ban lãnh đạo, BMP có lợi thế vì nhập nguyên liệu từ công ty mẹ 
là hệ sinh thái SCG tại Việt Nam và đối tác Nhật Bản là AGC Chemicals Vietnam, 
giúp giảm được 5% thuế nhập khẩu và chi phí vận chuyển. Điều này giúp BMP 
hưởng lợi kép khi có thể tăng được giá bán theo thị trường nhưng nguyên vật 
liệu rẻ hơn so với các doanh nghiệp trong ngành. KBSV nhận thấy định hướng 
dài hạn của BMP là cân bằng lợi ích cổ đông và khách hàng, vì vậy việc giảm giá 
bán để giành thị phần không phải lựa chọn ưu tiên của doanh nghiệp mà là 
nâng cao tỷ suất lợi nhuận/sản phẩm thành phẩm.

KBSV dự báo biên lợi nhuận gộp thu hẹp trong 2H2026 khi chi phí nguyên liệu 
giá cao từ 1H2026 được phản ánh vào giá vốn, và hồi phục trong 2027–2028 
nhờ đặc tính giữ giá bán ít thay đổi của nhóm nhựa. Chúng tôi kỳ vọng giá PVC 
resin chịu áp lực giảm từ (1) nhu cầu xây dựng tại Trung Quốc còn yếu khi thị 
trường bất động sản chưa hồi phục rõ ràng; và (2) các nhà sản xuất giải phóng 
hàng tồn kho sau mùa mưa, làm tăng sản lượng hạt nhựa PVC resin. Theo đó, 
KBSV dự phóng biên lợi nhuận gộp của BMP lên 46.4%/46.7%/46.5% trong 
2026/2027/2028.

gộp của BMP

Dự phóng kết quả kinh doanh

Document 286: Eurozone_ Improving business sentiment, driven by tech, is spreading to other sectors (2026-10-14)

Status: ok

### Page 1

14 October 2026
The bank
for a changing
world
EUROZONE: IMPROVING BUSINESS SENTIMENT, DRIVEN BY TECH, IS SPREADING TO OTHER SECTORS
Stéphane Colliac & Tarik Rharrab
*LATEST OBSERVATION: 5 OCTOBER 2026  **ESTIMATE
The September PMI surveys confirm that growth in the Eurozone is getting stronger, boosted in particular by new export orders, which are
still rising. Sectors that were already performing well – technology and capital goods (including for defence) – are continuing to grow, while

those that had been struggling until now – aeronautics, consumer goods and financial services – are seeing their production indices recover.
This strong performance is particularly noteworthy, given that it has not yet been undermined by higher inflation.
The heatmap shows the PMI index readings: a reading above 50 indicates expansion, while a reading below 50 signals contraction compared to the previous
month. For each indicator, sectors are ranked in descending order based on their September 2026 reading.

Shades of red represent values above 50, while shades of blue represent values below 50: the intensity of the colour increases the further away they are
from this threshold.
Production growth spreads to more sectors
The PMI surveys on business sentiment confirm that business activity in the Eurozone is expanding at a faster pace and spreading to more sectors.
Household-consumption-related sectors (goods and services), which were adversely affected in the spring by the outbreak of the conflict in the Middle

East, do not appear to be negatively impacted by the rising inflation observed since August. The aeronautics sector, where production has mainly been
limited by supply constraints, is also showing signs of improvement.
SOURCE: S&P GLOBAL, BNP PARIBAS
Eurozone sectoral PMI indices: production and new export orders are strengthening across more sectors

Chart of the Week / 14 October 2026 economic-research.bnpparibas.com
These trends, although still moderate, come alongside a drive to upgrade equipment, fuelled by investment in digital technology, electrification
(which is supporting the automotive sector) and defence. The momentum in technology goods and services further strengthened in September.
The production index for computer and electronics hit a five-year high, while the software services production index stayed close to its July peak.

Financial services are also enjoying a significant improvement, reflecting the healthy lending momentum despite interest rates starting to rise
(see our analysis).
Growth driven by exports
Exports drove growth in the first half of 2026, both across the Eurozone as a whole (see our analysis) and among its key members (notably
Germany). This momentum is also increasing, as indicated by the ‘new export orders’ component of the various PMI indices. As was the case with

production, there has been a particularly noteworthy increase for capital goods (technology and machinery) and related services, particularly digital
services. This trend is playing out more broadly, as, in September, new export orders rose across all sub-sectors, with the exception of intermediate
goods and consumer goods, where contraction recently eased. There has been a significant improvement in the transport sector, particularly in
the aeronautics industry, which has major markets in Asia. Recent data on French exports show that aeronautics exports to this region are rising

once again.
The convergence between rising production and increasing export-order backlogs shows that Eurozone growth is based on an increasingly solid
foundation. Inflationary pressures are not holding it back for the time being.
stephane.colliac@bnpparibas.com
tarik.rharrab@bnpparibas.com

Document 295: Five ways the UK’s Autumn Budget could shock us

Source PDF: all report pdf/Five ways the UK’s Autumn Budget could shock us.pdf
Report source: ING Think
Date: 2026-10-16
Pages: 10
Status: ok

### Page 1

From better ﬁscal headroom to surprise income tax hikes, this
is how UK Chancellor John Healey's ﬁrst budget could surprise
investors
James Smith
UK Chancellor John Healey
In a nutshell: What we expect
Higher debt interest costs look set to drive the level of 'ﬁscal
headroom' from £24bn in March to £10bn. There's a growing
sense that Chancellor John Healey would like to get this up
to £15bn or higher. The government is set to modestly
increase spending on the cost of living, though the total

intervention is unlikely to meaningfully top £5bn and it's
likely to be mostly billed as temporary to avoid counting
against the main ﬁscal rule. We expect an increase in capital
spending of roughly £10bn/year, bearing in mind this is
exempt from the ﬁscal rule dictating that the current budget
must be in surplus after ﬁve years. To rebuild headroom

### Page 2

against that rule, we expect £5-6bn of tax hikes, likely via a
higher Bank Surcharge and possibly broadening the National
Insurance tax base.
In short, we don't expect the budget to deliver a material
stimulus (or add materially to the already-planned ﬁscal
consolidation). The impact on the Bank of England, which is
poised to hike rates in November, is likely to be fairly
minimal.
But all chancellors like to keep a rabbit in their hat – and what
follows are ﬁve ways it could surprise us.

1. Fiscal headroom goes up – not down
What if the UK’s ﬁscal hole isn’t nearly as bad as we think?
The widespread expectation going into the UK’s Autumn Budget on
28 October is that the Chancellor is going to have to face up to
higher debt interest costs with some modest tax hikes.
Bank of England rate expectations are almost a percentage point
higher than they were at the time of the Oﬃce for Budget
Responsibility’s March assessment of Britain’s public ﬁnances. 10-

year bond yields are some 70 basis points higher, which, taken
together, will push up debt interest spending by £11bn/year by
2029/30.
That points to a sizeable reduction in the amount of money the
government has left under its main ﬁscal rule, which requires the
current budget (day-to-day spending, including debt interest less
revenue) to be in surplus within three years. Back in March, that
surplus, or ﬁscal headroom, was projected at £24bn in 2029/30.

Debt interest costs are rising

### Page 3

*Based on the average rate in the 10 days up to 25 September, which

Source: Macrobond, ING
We think that headroom will have dropped to around £10bn before
any corrective action. And the vibe from the Treasury suggests the
Chancellor would prefer it to be above £15bn, to give more
breathing space in case of future adverse forecast revisions. That
would point to some limited tax hikes.
But what if that’s all wrong? The truth is, nobody really knows how

the OBR will change its assessment of the UK economy. And as we
saw last year, the Treasury is often inclined to talk up the doom and
gloom to open up a positive surprise on the day. 12 months ago,
the hit to headroom was considerably smaller than had been
widely expected.
This time, any positive surprise is most likely to come from wage
growth. Will the rise in energy prices spill into higher pay, just as it
did in 2022? We think it won’t, not least because the jobs market is

much weaker, an assessment we suspect the OBR will share.
But even relatively small upgrades to wage growth can make a big
diﬀerence to the government’s fortunes. Since 2010, the OBR has
revised the level of earnings across its ﬁve-year forecast by an
average of 1.3 percentage points in absolute terms. If it did that
now, say by lifting wage growth over the next two years by 0.6ppt
apiece, it would add £10bn to headroom, almost perfectly
oﬀsetting the hit from higher interest costs.

### Page 4

There are also reasons to think the impact of lower migration
projections, which some reports suggest could wipe another £7bn
oﬀ headroom via lower tax revenue forecasts, could be much less
than feared. The starting point for employment looks a little higher
than it did back in March. The rise in global equities this year should
also be helpful for capital gains tax projections.
Why does this all matter? Because the higher the headroom, the
less ‘need’ there is to hike taxes, and if it is signiﬁcantly higher than

expected, the more room there is to increase spending under the
ﬁscal rules.
The simple message is this: don’t judge the Autumn Budget by the
level of headroom left over.
2. Spending splurge
The problem with ‘headroom’ is not only that it’s highly sensitive to
the OBR’s economic outlook, but that the forward-looking nature of
the ﬁscal rules opens the door to a lot of creative accounting.
The impact of higher inﬂation is a case in point. This helps the

Treasury through higher receipts – both VAT and income tax. Yes, it
also pushes up the cost of indexed beneﬁts. But importantly,
departmental budgets don’t automatically increase to oﬀset the
impact of higher prices.
That opens up a classic trick from the Treasury, which is to leave
spending plans virtually unchanged in nominal terms, setting up
substantially smaller real-terms budget increases across the
medium term.
Even before the Iran War, government plans pencilled in real-terms

cuts to per capita departmental spending in FY2029. Given some
functions, like health, are ‘protected’, that implies much deeper cuts
to smaller departments.
Suﬃce it to say, that is unrealistic. Higher inﬂation will push up the
wage bill across government. It would cost £12bn to simply
maintain those March departmental spending plans in real terms.
That’s before considering the cost of ending that austerity pencilled

### Page 5

in for later this decade, or how the government will fund its defence
or social care ambitions.
A top-up to government departments is inevitable – the question is
when. It looks like the Treasury wants to kick the can on these tricky
budget decisions until next autumn, when it plans to release its
next Spending Review, a process of allocating money across
departments. No doubt the hope is that debt interest costs will
have fallen and the economic outlook brightened, such that there's

more space under the ﬁscal rules to make bigger spending
commitments.
That points to a very modest package of spending measures this
month, mainly targeting the cost of living. On top of the already-
announced cut to VAT on energy, we’d imagine the Treasury will
replicate last year’s budget and lower household bills by funding a
greater share of ‘policy costs’ from general taxation. This has the
added beneﬁt of lowering headline inﬂation at a particularly

sensitive moment for the Bank of England. Crucially though, the
government is likely to bill these moves as temporary, so they don’t
count under that main ﬁscal rule.
But if the economic picture really is better than feared, don’t rule
out the government going further. A decision to fully fund the UK’s
3% defence spending commitment would be a major wildcard,
though this would likely come at the expense of capital plans in
other areas of government.

Ultimately, the key thing to watch is the OBR’s assessment of how
all these policy changes aﬀect GDP growth and borrowing over the
next year. If there’s a material stimulus eﬀect, it could add to
market bets on UK rate hikes.
3. A manifesto break on income tax
Tax hikes are likely at the Autumn Budget, but should be very small.
We’re probably looking at a package of circa £5bn (0.2% of GDP).
Part of the problem is that the list of viable options looks perilously

small if Labour is to avoid breaking its manifesto pledge not to raise

Is there scope for a surprise? We think there are two main ways on
tax – one good, one bad.
The good? A surprise announcement of a so-called ‘tax switch’, a
novel proposal from Resolution Foundation which would see the
government simultaneously raise basic income tax and cut

employee National Insurance by 2ppt. The impact on workers
would be neutral, but because income tax has a broader base
(including pensions), this would raise £6bn/year.
Though not a substantial sum, it would signal to investors that
Prime Minister Andy Burnham isn’t afraid to go near the thorny
issue of raising taxes. But in light of recent pension changes,

including to the infamous ‘triple lock’, this feels like too much of a
political gamble at this stage.
The negative surprise would be if the Treasury decided to raise the
basic tax allowance. Burnham ﬂirted with this on his ﬁrst day in
oﬃce; it would not only be expensive (potentially £10bn), but it
would also signal that the UK’s commitment to the threshold freeze
– which is doing the lion's share of the planned ﬁscal consolidation
– is waning.
A big rise in the National Living Wage

The minimum wage increase in April 2027, which will be
announced alongside the budget, is perhaps the most important
variable for the Bank of England.
This is based on a recommendation from the Low Pay Commission,
which earlier this year provisionally estimated the increase at 3.7%.
That’s a smaller increase than over the past two years and,
crucially, is not a million miles above the level the Bank considers
consistent with a 2% medium-term inﬂation target (3.25%).

But the LPC has some ﬂexibility in its recommendation, and one
criterion is the cost of living. Higher inﬂation means we could see a
ﬁnal proposal between 4% and 4.5%. Anything above that would,
we suspect, be problematic for the Bank of England and would
bolster the case for higher rates.
The government is no doubt well aware of this. And there appears
to be growing recognition among Labour ﬁgures, including on the
left of the party, that the big minimum wage increases, coupled

with previous payroll tax increases, have had a big impact on
business – particularly in consumer services.
But if the LPC were to recommend minimum wage growth of close
to 5%, could the government resist it? In theory, yes. But politically,
we suspect not.
A surge in bond issuance
We already know that bond issuance is set to increase again in
2027, though for reasons unrelated to the UK’s ﬁscal trajectory.

据韩国《东亚⽇报》援引投资银⾏业知情⼈⼠报道， SK 海⼒⼠计划派发约 16 万亿韩元（约 121
亿美元）特别股利，资⾦来源为出售⽇本存储芯⽚制造商铠侠（ Kioxia ）股权所获收益。
该计划预计将于 10 ⽉ 28 ⽇（周三） SK 海⼒⼠公布第三季财报时正式对外宣布。
据估计， SK 海⼒⼠在第⼀季及第⼆季陆续出售铠侠持股，扣除原始投资成本后，合计净利约达
20 万亿韩元（约合  151 亿美元）。

此次拟派发的 16 万亿韩元特别股利，约相当于上述净利的⼋成。

Document 388: Top of Mind - Assessing a Less Transparent Fed

Source PDF: all report pdf/Top of Mind - Assessing a Less Transparent Fed.pdf
Report source: Goldman Sachs (Top of Mind)
Date: 2026-08-25
Pages: 26
Status: ok

### Page 1

The Goldman Sachs Group, Inc.
Fed Chairman Kevin Warsh is steering the Fed into a less transparent era. So, is that
a positive or negative development, and what could it mean for markets and the
economy? We speak with Former Fed governors Donald Kohn and Stephen Miran
as well as GS’ Jan Hatzius who agree that rigid “Odyssean” forward guidance that
commits policymakers to a pre-set path risks policy mistakes and is therefore rarely
appropriate. But they somewhat disagree about the benefits of great er transparency

around the Fed’s reaction function. Hatzius and Kohn argue that such transparency
helps markets better anticipate and price in policy changes, accelerating monetary
policy transmission. But Miran believes that the line between committing to a path
and being transparent is clearer in theory than in practice. And while GS economists
and strategists find that reduc ed transparency leads to increased market volatility,
the question is whether this just increases the market’s noise or also improves the

market’s signal. Hatzius argues largely for the former and Miran for the latter.
Jan Hatzius, Chief Economist and Head of Global Investment Research, Goldman Sachs
“Markets price what they think the Fed will do, not what they think the Fed should do. That will remain true even if the Fed
obscures its reaction function… market guesses about the Fed’s next moves will simply become worse, leading to more
volatility... and importantly, volatility that serves no constructive economic purpose.”

Donald Kohn, former Vice Chairman of the Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System, Robert V . Roosa
Chair in International Economics and Senior Fellow, Brookings Institution
“I sympathize with Chairman Warsh to some extent. [But] the real problem with Warsh’s approach is his reluctance to articulate
how the FOMC sees the economy evolving… explaining the Fed’s thinking about the economic outlook is essential in helping
markets act as a stabilizing force and is a core component of accountability.”

### Page 3

El
Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research 3 
Top of Mind Issue 151

New Fed Chairman Kevin Warsh is steering the Fed into a less 
transparent era characterized by shorter post-meeting 
statements, less forward guidance, and a more limited role for 
projections, marking a meaningful break from the 
transparency revolution that has defined central banking over 
the last quarter century. So, what could a less transparent Fed 
mean for markets and the economy? 
For answers, we turn to former Fed governors Donald Kohn, 
now Robert V. Roosa Chair in International Economics and 
senior fellow at the Brookings Institution, and Stephen Miran, 
now senior strategist at Hudson Bay Capital Management, as 
well as Jan Hatzius, GS’ Chief Economist and Head of Global 
Investment Research. 
We first ask whether scaling back Fed communication is a 
positive or negative development. Hatzius says it’s likely 
negative, though he draws a clear line between transparency 
and forward guidance. He argues that clarity about the Fed’s 
reaction function helps markets better anticipate policy 
changes and quickly incorporate new information into financial 
conditions, thereby accelerating monetary policy transmission 
to the economy. So, he believes that unwinding that 
transparency would be a mistake. (Note: GS Global 
Economists Joseph Briggs and Megan Peters lay out the 
evidence backing this argument on pgs. 14-15.) 
But Hatzius takes a more nuanced view of forward guidance, 
arguing that rigid “Odyssean” guidance that commits 
policymakers to a pre-set path regardless of new information 
only makes sense at the effective lower bound for the policy 
rate, while conditional “Delphic” guidance tied to the 
economic outlook is generally more defensible. Although 
markets could confuse the two, he believes that most 
sophisticated investors understand the difference and is 
therefore not particularly worried that markets have become 
overly reliant on the policy dots published in the Fed’s 
Summary of Economic Projections (SEP). Instead, he 
considers them a “useful source of additional information 
about the Fed’s reaction function” (except for the long-run 
policy dot). If anything, Hatzius would prefer the dots move 
in an even more informative direction—by publishing each 
policymaker’s combined projections for growth, inflation, 
unemployment, and the policy rate on an anonymized basis. 
Kohn agrees that clarity about the Fed’s “narrative” and 
reaction function is vital. Explaining how the Fed sees the 
economy evolving and how policy would respond, he says, is 
“essential in helping markets act as a stabilizing force and is 
a core component of accountability.” Still, he sympathizes 
somewhat with Chairman Warsh’s view that the dot plot is 
“not helpful” (see pg. 9 for Warsh’s own words on this), 
arguing that the market’s hyper-focus on the SEP’s median 
policy dot “illustrates the risk of excessive precision and 
false certainty,” as small shifts in it can trigger unwarranted 
market moves. So, he has long favored omitting the median. 
Miran, though, is more supportive of Warsh’s push for a less 
transparent Fed. Like Hatzius and Kohn, he’s concerned that 
excessive forward guidance that locks the Fed into a policy 
path raises the risk of policy errors and also argues that such 
guidance can impair market pricing of risk. But he’s less 
convinced about the benefits of increased transparency 
around the Fed’s reaction function, making the case that the 
distinction between the Fed committing to a pre-set policy 
path and being transparent about its reaction function is clear 
in theory, but less so in practice. So, he thinks “the policy dots 
absolutely have to go” but feels less strongly about omitting 
the SEP’s economic projections. 
But would less transparency allow markets to provide the Fed 
with more “direct and unfiltered” information about the 
economy, as Warsh has argued? The answer seems to boil 
down to the implications for market volatility, and whether any 
resulting volatility would simply increase the market’s noise or 
also improve its signal. 
As for the volatility implications, the evidence is fairly clear. 
Briggs and Peters find that increased central bank 
transparency has led to a reduction in market volatility over the 
last several decades. This is consistent with GS Head of US 
Rates Strategy William Marshall’s recent observation of the 
reverse: that the US rates market has already priced 
substantial uncertainty associated with a less transparent Fed, 
with the July FOMC representing the largest non-cut meeting 
day surprise in almost three decades.  
But Marshall argues that whether such meeting-day surprises 
lead to structurally higher rates volatility depends (again) on 
whether the Fed’s reaction function is well understood. If it’s 
not, he would expect higher rates volatility to persist. GS FX 
Strategists Michael Cahill and Lexi Kanter find a similar 
pattern in FX: that central bank communication shifts can 
change which events and data drive FX volatility without 
necessarily raising it, but less transparency could increase 
Dollar volatility—all else equal (though global macro 
conditions remain its biggest driver).  
But the key debate seems to be whether the increased 
volatility resulting from less Fed transparency will improve or 
worsen the market’s signal. Hatzius is clearly in the “worsen” 
camp. He argues that markets price what they think the Fed 
will do—not what it should do—and less transparency won’t 
change that; it would simply produce worse guesses. So, he 
says that while volatility in and of itself is not necessarily bad, 
volatility driven by uncertainty about the Fed’s reaction 
function would serve no constructive economic purpose. 
Kohn agrees that the lack of a clear Fed narrative could create 
damaging uncertainty. But he argues that removing overly 
specific guidance could help free markets to process new 
information more independently and isn’t too worried about 
modestly higher volatility as a result. And Miran seems most 
convinced that reducing transparency would improve the 
markets’ signal, with volatility an unavoidable but acceptable 
cost: “The first best world of signal without volatility doesn't 
exist. I'd rather have that signal, even if it's messy, than 
throw the signal out by anchoring market expectations to the 
Fed's near-term projections.”

Assessing less Fed transparency

### Page 4

El
Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research 4 
Top of Mind Issue 151

Jan Hatzius is Chief Economist and Head of Global Investment Research at Goldman Sachs. 
Below, he argues that a shift toward a less transparent Fed is likely a negative development.  
Ashley Rhodes: Fed Chairman Kevin 
Warsh appears to be ushering the 
Fed into a less transparent era. Is 
that shift a positive or negative 
development? 
Jan Hatzius: It’s likely a negative 
development, although I would 
distinguish between transparency and 
forward guidance. The transparency 
revolution in central banking over the last 30 years has been 
extremely successful. When a central bank provides 
meaningful information about its reaction function—how it’s 
likely to respond to different economic outcomes—markets can 
better anticipate policy changes. So, new information gets 
incorporated quickly into financial conditions, accelerating the 
transmission of monetary policy. The central bank can later 
decide whether to ratify that move and always retains the 
option not to. That framework has worked well for the Fed over 
the past several decades. Unwinding transparency about the 
reaction function risks delaying the transmission of monetary 
policy and would be a mistake, in my view. 
Forward guidance about the policy rate path is a separate and 
more nuanced issue. In assessing the pros and cons, it’s 
important to distinguish between “Odyssean” and “Delphic” 
forward guidance. With Odyssean guidance, like Odysseus 
tying himself to the mast, the central bank commits to a policy 
path even if subsequent data point in a different direction. That 
only makes sense at the effective lower bound for the policy 
rate, when conventional monetary easing is no longer an 
option. Even then, the hurdle for Odyssean guidance should be 
high because there is a real risk that policymakers lock 
themselves into an inappropriate policy stance. By contrast, 
Delphic guidance, named for the Oracle of Delphi, is a 
conditional description of what policymakers expect to do given 
their economic outlook that will change with new information. 
Delphic guidance is more defensible, even in normal times. 
Ashley Rhodes: Let’s take these in turn. Do you agree with 
the argument that Odyssean forward guidance increases 
the risk of policy errors? 
Jan Hatzius: That argument is a serious one. In terms of 
practical examples, it is heavily centered on the 2021–2022 
episode, when the Fed tightened policy much too late because 
they made a major forecasting error on inflation at a time of 
rigid Odyssean forward guidance. That was a bad combination. 
If the forecast is wrong, policy will be set incorrectly under any 
communication or transparency framework. And Odyssean 
forward guidance compounds that problem because it delays 
the inevitable course correction. This is why Odyssean forward 
guidance should be used sparingly, if at all. 
That said, I am not sure how big a difference to the inflation 
outcome or to economic performance more broadly it would 
have made if the Fed had started to tighten in, say, September 
2021 rather than March 2022. By early 2022, markets had 
already recognized that the funds rate would need to rise, and 
financial conditions tightened substantially on the back of 
higher rate expectations well before the Fed delivered those 
hikes. Also, the error was ultimately not disastrous 
economically; inflation expectations did not de-anchor and the 
Fed course-corrected without causing a recession. 
Ashley Rhodes: Even if the Fed just provides Delphic 
guidance, are concerns that the market might confuse it 
with Odyssean guidance warranted, especially given how 
much weight markets put on the dot plot? 
Jan Hatzius: Such confusion would be problematic, but I think 
it doesn’t happen frequently in practice. Most sophisticated 
observers understand that the policy dots included in the 
Summary of Economic Projections (SEP) are conditional on data 
evolving in line with the economic projections. And the dots 
only provide information about the individual and anonymized 
views of FOMC participants, including non-voters. So, they 
aren’t strong forward guidance, not even strong Delphic 
forward guidance. And market expectations have often 
diverged meaningfully from them, undermining the argument 
that investors view them as a commitment.  
Instead, the dots are a useful source of additional information 
about the Fed’s reaction function. The SEP could be made even 
more informative by publishing each policymaker’s combined 
projections for growth, inflation, unemployment, and the Fed 
funds rate on an anonymous basis, which we have long argued 
for. Doing so would provide a clearer picture of how 
policymakers’ economic forecasts relate to their anticipated 
policy paths. But that seems unlikely given Warsh’s apparent 
preference for moving in the opposite direction. 
Concerns about market confusion are more understandable 
when it comes to the long-run policy dot, where the Fed has 
less information to convey. Nobody knows where R-star is—as 
Chair Powell liked to say, “it is seen by its works”—but 
markets seem to assume that the Fed has special knowledge 
and therefore place too much weight on the long-term dot. 
That can lock down longer-term rate expectations too firmly. 
That said, removing the long-run dot could complicate the Fed’s 
communication in its current form, as it has become an 
important benchmark for assessing the stance of monetary 
policy. I would prefer to eliminate the long-run dot and 
communicate the stance of policy with greater emphasis on 
financial conditions. Overall, though, the dot plot works well, 
and, despite the controversy surrounding it, I remain a fan. 
Ashley Rhodes: Aren’t the Fed’s inflation and employment 
targets sufficient for the market to infer the Fed’s reaction? 
Jan Hatzius: No, because getting from point A to point B 
requires more information than just a stated objective. Take the 
inflation target, for example. The Fed's goal is 2% inflation, but 
how do you assess whether inflation is moving sustainably 
toward that target? How do you determine the current 
underlying rate of inflation? Should you look at headline PCE, 
core PCE, trimmed mean inflation, or something else entirely? 
Do you project future inflation changes based on the 
Interview with Jan Hatzius

### Page 5

El
Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research 5
Top of Mind Issue 151
unemployment rate via a Phillips curve relationship, the
monetary aggregates, or something else? To anticipate the
Fed’s moves—and thereby push financial conditions in a
stabilizing direction—markets need insight into how the Fed
assesses these questions. Those considerations go well
beyond stating a broad policy goal.
Ashley Rhodes: Is there any merit to communicating less
given that Fed officials have, at times, inadvertently made

market-moving remarks?
Jan Hatzius: There is certainly an optimal level and frequency
of communication. The Fed should not provide running
commentary on every data release, especially as markets tend
to overemphasize individual data points relative to the longer-
term trend. The Reserve Bank presidents speak a lot more
frequently than the governors and their comments are almost
by definition less informative about the policy path than those
of the Fed leadership. While that can complicate the public's

understanding of the policy outlook, it can also be informative
for market participants who nevertheless understand that the
views of the troika—the Fed chairman, Fed vice chair, and New
York Fed president—carry the greatest weight. On net, it’s
therefore unclear whether noise from frequent policymaker
comments has had big practical costs. More broadly,
communication mistakes are inevitable, and policymakers will
say things they later wish they hadn't. But the question is

whether these occasional mistakes outweigh the benefits from
transparent communication. In my view, they don’t.
Ashley Rhodes: Warsh believes that providing less
guidance about the Fed’s reaction function will allow
policymakers to draw more “direct and unfiltered”
information from markets. So, you don’t agree?
Jan Hatzius: I don’t. Markets price what they think the Fed will
do, not what they think the Fed should do. That will remain true
even if the Fed obscures its reaction function. So, if the Fed

chooses to do so, market guesses about the Fed’s next moves
will simply become worse, leading to more volatility in rates
markets and in financial conditions—and importantly, volatility
that serves no constructive economic purpose. Volatility is not
bad in and of itself. For example, if new data shows increased
inflation risks, financial conditions should tighten as markets
price in rate hikes. This accelerates the transmission of
monetary policy. But volatility driven by uncertainty about the

Fed’s reaction function is more random and doesn’t necessarily
push financial conditions in a stabilizing direction.
Also, policymakers can already extract valuable information
from market indicators. For example, a sharp rise in breakeven
inflation above target is a statement that markets view the
expected policy path as inappropriately loose. So, the
information is already available without the Fed saying less.
Ashley Rhodes: Is there a risk of a “hall of mirrors” effect if

the Fed uses market prices to not only glean information
about the economy but also to guide what it should do?
Jan Hatzius: Yes, but I am not particularly concerned about
this risk. While a “hall of mirrors” effect—whereby markets
watch the Fed for clues, but the Fed watches the markets for
clues—could push the policy stance far away from desirable
levels, the likelihood of that scenario materializing is low.
Warsh has said that he does not want to “be constrained or

take verbatim from what the market’s doing.” And Fed
officials, including the Chairman, are unlikely to take that logic
to its extreme conclusion and follow the market simply
because it has priced in a particular policy move.
Ashley Rhodes: Warsh has also said that financial markets
“are probably the most important source of information to
guide central bankers.” Do you agree?
Jan Hatzius: Not entirely, as I would rank the economic data
first. That said, financial market prices are very important, and

central banks have become increasingly sophisticated in
extracting information from them. There’s always room for
improvement, but again, the information is already available
under the current regime. And communicating less
transparently wouldn’t help policymakers evaluate financial
market signals more appropriately.
Ashley Rhodes: How durable is a less transparent Fed?
Jan Hatzius: That remains an open question. As Chairman,
Warsh has significant control over the Fed’s communications

framework and can choose to provide less information at press
conferences, as he has done. In that sense, a less transparent
approach could prove durable, though its consequences remain
to be seen. Other communication changes, such as eliminating
the dot plot, would probably in practice require FOMC support,
and my sense is that other Fed officials would see that as too
great a step away from transparency.
A large reduction in Fed communication, defined by the

number of speeches, is less likely to endure. Reserve Bank
presidents have strong incentives to continue talking, in part
because the influence and relevance of their institutions
depend on it. This has been a perennial issue—Chairman Alan
Greenspan was often very frustrated with the Bank presidents.
Warsh could try to rein them in. But even Greenspan in his
heyday failed to do so. If regional bank presidents continue
speaking frequently while centralized communication declines,

the cacophony could actually worsen.
Ashley Rhodes: More broadly, while the Fed held rates
steady in July, three FOMC participants dissented in favor
of a hike, fueling debate about whether further tightening
is on the horizon. Where do you stand?
Jan Hatzius: We don’t expect the Fed to hike, largely because
we think much of the recent strength in core PCE inflation
reflects temporary factors: higher oil prices related to the US-
Iran war, roughly 70bp of lingering tariff pass-through, and

distortions in software and accessories prices. As these effects
fade, we expect core inflation to move close to 2% in 2027.
That assessment is consistent with the ongoing deceleration in
other measures of underlying inflation such as trimmed-mean
PCE and core CPI.
The recent data are also broadly consistent with that view. The
inflation data improved significantly in June and July, and the
labor market reports for both months were somewhat weaker

on balance. If those trends continue, policymakers will likely
conclude that additional tightening is unnecessary, especially
with the funds rate already around 3.5-3.75%—a level the Fed
likely views as roughly neutral or even slightly restrictive.

Donald Kohn is former Vice Chairman of the Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve 
System and current Robert V. Roosa Chair in International Economics and senior fellow at the 
Brookings Institution. Below, he sympathizes with Warsh’s desire to scale back forward 
guidance but sees a bigger issue with his reluctance to explain the Fed’s reaction function.  
The views stated herein are those of the interviewee and do not necessarily reflect those of Goldman Sachs.
Jenny Grimberg: You saw firsthand 
the Fed’s evolution from a relatively 
opaque institution into one that 
communicates through statements, 
press conferences, projections, and 
public remarks. What drove this 
shift toward greater transparency?    
Donald Kohn: A key motivation was 
to get financial markets on the Fed’s 
side. While the Fed controls the overnight rate, which acts as 
the anchor for the short end of the yield curve, monetary policy 
transmission to the real economy works through broad financial 
conditions, including equity prices, the Dollar, and long-term 
yields. The Fed believed that the more the market understands 
its policy framework and objectives, the more it will work with, 
rather than against, the Fed—as new data arrives, markets will 
adjust in a stabilizing direction. The second motivation was 
accountability. The Fed benefits from being an independent 
institution largely insulated from the political process, but that 
independence comes with a responsibility to provide the public 
and Congress a clear explanation of its actions.  
Jenny Grimberg: Why is it so important for markets to 
work with the Fed? 
Donald Kohn: Markets work better and reinforce central bank 
policies more effectively when they understand how 
policymakers expect the economy to evolve and how policy will 
likely respond. Historically, a lack of explicit communication has 
sometimes caused costly misunderstandings. Prior to 1994, 
the Fed didn’t announce its policy decisions; markets had to 
infer them from the New York Fed’s open market operations. 
An infamous misunderstanding known as the “Thanksgiving 
turkey” occurred in November 1989, when markets 
misconstrued a routine reserve injection as policy easing. The 
resulting volatility prompted an emergency FOMC phone call 
where policymakers had to decide whether to validate the 
market’s mistaken interpretation by cutting rates or maintain 
the existing policy stance, ultimately choosing the latter.  
In 1994, the Fed introduced explicit policy announcements, 
which grew more complex over time by, for example, including 
a policy “tilt” to signal whether policy was more likely to 
tighten or loosen. This toolkit proved vital in the early 2000s, 
when markets prematurely priced in rate hikes, causing 
financial conditions to tighten faster than was consistent with 
the FOMC’s objectives; the Fed was able to explain its thinking 
more clearly, successfully realigning expectations.  
Jenny Grimberg: Which communication innovation has 
made the greatest practical difference? 
Donald Kohn: The innovations we’ve discussed, as well as the 
move to increase the frequency of the publication of the Fed’s 
economic forecasts from semi-annually to quarterly, were all 
important. But if I had to choose the single most important 
innovation, it would be the post-FOMC meeting press 
conference, which Chairman Ben Bernanke, who had a strong 
predilection for increasing transparency, introduced in 2011. 
The press conference provides the chair with a direct, 
unfiltered opportunity to articulate the FOMC’s thinking and 
explain the rationale behind its decisions. That’s very powerful.    
Jenny Grimberg: Conversely, has history also provided 
lessons about the risks of greater transparency? 
Donald Kohn: The most serious risk is that forward guidance—
guidance about the likely future path of policy—can constrain 
both the FOMC’s and the market’s reaction to incoming data. A 
prominent example occurred during the Covid recovery, when 
the Fed committed to holding rates at zero until the economy 
reached full employment, almost regardless of the inflation 
rate. This “Odyssean” commitment effectively constrained the 
Fed’s response to emerging inflationary pressures.  
I’ve received blowback on this point from Fed officials who 
argue that the real problem was bad forecasts. It’s true that 
most forecasters, including the Fed, failed to anticipate the 
persistence of the price pressures. But the forecasts, the Fed’s 
Flexible Average Inflation Targeting Framework, and the 
resulting forward guidance were all born out of a 15-year 
mindset of combatting too-low inflation. So, the guidance was 
too constraining, even if the forecasts were also at fault. 
The market’s hyper-focus on the median forecast in the 
Summary of Economic Projections (SEP) also illustrates the risk 
of excessive precision and false certainty. This median is a very 
weak indicator of the FOMC’s collective stance; a move by just 
one or two participants can shift it, triggering unwarranted 
market reactions, which is partly why I’ve long advocated for 
stopping its publication.   
Jenny Grimberg: So, is Chairman Warsh’s move to reduce  
transparency a positive or negative shift? 
Donald Kohn: I sympathize with Chairman Warsh to some 
extent. Forward guidance with commitment is an important 
tool at the zero lower bound, even if the Fed’s pandemic-era 
Odyssean guidance was a poor choice. But once policy rates 
are well above zero, the need for it diminishes. At that point, 
the Fed has ample conventional policy space to adjust rates as 
economic conditions evolve. And, as we’ve discussed, 
projecting a specific number of rate hikes or cuts as in the 
policy dot plot is pretending to know more than policymakers 
actually do. So, I would be a reluctant user of forward guidance 
away from the zero lower bound—and with the Fed funds rate 
currently at 3.5-3.75%, we’re clearly far from it.  
The real problem with Warsh’s approach is his reluctance to 
articulate how the FOMC sees the economy evolving, 
Interview with Donald Kohn

### Page 7

El
Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research 7
Top of Mind Issue 151
seemingly out of fear that the market will interpret what he
says as a policy hint. But explaining the Fed’s thinking about
the economic outlook is essential in helping markets act as a
stabilizing force and is a core component of accountability.
Central banks need a narrative. Chairman Alan Greenspan,
whom Warsh has expressed a desire to emulate, always had a
story—even if it wasn’t always well understood—that was

backed by deep analysis, and when economic developments
diverged from expectations, Greenspan could investigate why
the data weren’t aligning. So, a narrative is crucial.
That said, I see merit in some of Warsh’s other ideas, like
reducing the number of FOMC meetings. While he hasn’t
specified a target, a move from eight to six meetings a year
would allow policymakers to evaluate two months of economic
data, encouraging the FOMC and markets to focus on trends

rather than individual data points. So, I would support such a
move, provided it doesn’t disrupt the flow of information from
the Fed staff to policymakers or increase intermeeting actions.
Jenny Grimberg: Warsh seems to want markets to “play
the ball, not the referee,” enabling the Fed to obtain more
“direct and unfiltered” information about the economy
from markets. How viable is such a feedback loop?
Donald Kohn: Markets will always react to new information

based partly on how they expect the Fed to react. While Warsh
has urged market participants to focus on the ball rather than
the referee, the Fed is not the referee—it’s the 800-pound
gorilla in the rates markets. So, the market will always interpret
incoming data through the lens of expected Fed policy.
But a golden mean exists. Overly specific forward guidance can
potentially damp the market’s reaction to incoming data, so
backing off from such specificity could help free markets to

process new information more independently. But the Fed
must still provide its narrative—explaining how it’s thinking and
what it’s focused on—to help the market figure out the
appropriate rate path to achieve the Fed’s objectives.
Jenny Grimberg: Curtailing guidance would likely increase
short-term market volatility. Does that concern you?
Donald Kohn: Not necessarily. If forward guidance has indeed
suppressed market reactions, removing it should improve the

quality of the signal. So, a modest increase in short-term
volatility is acceptable, provided the Fed equips market
participants with enough information to react intelligently to
incoming data. But an important distinction exists between
productive volatility and harmful uncertainty. Creating
uncertainty for its own sake is counterproductive and can
dampen investment, and failing to articulate a clear story
generates this damaging uncertainty. If the Chairman doesn’t

tell that story, dissenting policymakers may step in to fill the
void—as occurred after the recent FOMC meeting—confusing
the market and obscuring the actual policy consensus.
Jenny Grimberg: Warsh has also said that financial
markets “are probably the most important source of
information to guide central bankers.” Do you agree?
Donald Kohn: I’m not sure they’re the most important, but
they’re certainly very important. When I was at the Fed, I
looked carefully at what the markets were saying about what

they thought we should do, what they were reacting to, and
whether that reaction was appropriate. But the Fed must
interpret markets relative to what markets expect the Fed to
do. Otherwise, policymakers risk the “monkey in the mirror”
problem—watching the reflection of their own actions and
adjusting policy to match owing to the mistaken belief that the
market is providing independent validation. Navigating this
issue requires a delicate balance. The more accurate markets’

reactions are, the more they’ll behave in a stabilizing manner,
which, as we’ve discussed, was a key driver behind the Fed’s
shift toward greater transparency. But Warsh is also correct
that forward guidance can become too prescriptive, resulting in
market pricing simply reflecting the FOMC’s past guidance and
obscuring the informational value of the signal. So, while
market prices are an invaluable source of information for central
bankers, interpreting them is undoubtedly a tricky game.

Jenny Grimberg: Ultimately, how durable is the quieter
communication regime that Warsh has ushered in?
Donald Kohn: Warsh’s level of quietness is likely
unsustainable. The market reaction to his July press
conference—declining short-term rates and rising long-term
rates—was undoubtedly not the outcome he desired. I sat next
to him for nearly four years at the Fed, and I know him to be a
highly capable policymaker. So, I’m confident he’ll take this

lesson on board and move in the direction of filling the current
communication void. I’m also confident that the
communications task force he’s established will help move him
in that direction, especially with Mervyn King co-leading it. King
revolutionized communication at the BoE by introducing
inflation projections and outlining the “key judgements” behind
the BoE’s forecasts, and in his book Radical Uncertainty
emphasized that central banks need a narrative built on

rigorous analysis that can be validated or contradicted by
incoming data.
Warsh has also talked about less discussion from the Reserve
Bank presidents. These policymakers are a vital liaison
between the Fed and local communities and so shouldn’t be
silenced. But the public would benefit from a different form of
communication from the Reserve Bank presidents—their public
remarks should explain the FOMC’s collective decisions and
broader economic trends rather than offer instant analysis of

individual data points or telegraph policy paths. So, the
objective shouldn’t be to restrict communication, but to foster
more substantive and less reactive dialogue.
Jenny Grimberg: Investors are also focused on the Fed’s
near-term policy path. What do you expect the Fed’s next
move will be?
Donald Kohn: I wouldn’t have voted to raise rates in July given
that the June CPI print was favorable and long-term inflation
expectations seemed anchored. But if forced to say whether

the Fed’s next move is more likely to be a hike or a cut, I would
lean toward a hike. I am worried that inflation could be stuck at
2.5%, and because it has run above target for some time,
longer-term inflation expectations may be less firmly anchored
than usual. So, as a matter of risk management, buying
insurance against inflation running too high for too long seems
prudent. But if the favorable June, and now July, CPI prints
were followed by another favorable one in August, hiking in

September probably wouldn’t be appropriate.

Fed guidance, in Warsh’s own words 
“I understand the desire for rolling forecasts and
commentary from this Committee. But for our
part, we need to observe market reaction to
developments, direct and unfiltered… We're
trying not to interfere with that market signal.
That's part of the reason why we've been
somewhat spare on our words, when we pulled
back from forward guidance.”
- July 2026 FOMC press conference
Source: Federal Reserve, ECB, Congress.gov, The Structural Foundations of Monetary Policy, WSJ, compiled by Goldman Sachs GIR. 
“I think financial markets perform best when they
react to incoming data. I think they—the financial
markets work less efficiently when they ask a
question: How will the Federal Reserve react to
that incoming information? The more that
markets are paying attention to what’s happening
in the real economy, deciding what’s good data
and what’s less good data, the more financial
markets can price what they believe is the most
likely and what are the tail risks. Financial market
prices are probably the most important source of
information to guide central bankers.”
“This afternoon, you also received the usual
Summary of Economic Projections. It’s been the
practice of this Committee for participants to

- July 2026 FOMC press conference
“Market participants are learning to play the ball,
not the referee — and market prices will continue
to respond in the direction and magnitude they
see fit. This is, in my view, a change for the
better—and we are just getting started… By not
spoon-feeding markets, by not previewing our
decisions, by not sort of giving nudges and leans,
my colleagues and I have found in the
intermeeting period what we're getting is the

- From a chapter in “The Structural

“Financial markets and the real economy work
best when you look at what’s happening in the real
economy, you make your own judgments. There
has been a tendency, and I take plenty of blame
[for] this, the ‘08 crisis where we we’re trying to
suppress volatility, where we thought we needed
to spoon feed markets to get out of that. That was
the right policy for a crisis. It’s not the right policy
for the time that we have now. And so sometimes
unlearning is harder than learning and I’m going
to keep at it.”
- ECB Forum on Central Banking, July 2026
- June 2026 FOMC press conference
“In the monthly window between FOMC
meetings, policy makers provide the minutes of
the preceding meeting and make dozens of
statements, speeches, interviews, forecasts, and
other predictions. They pay great attention to the
latest payroll data and inflation prints and their
immediate effect on the next decision. There is,
however, little to be learned from the latest Fed
forecast, little insight to be gleaned from the last
piece of government data. I worry that Fed policy
makers find themselves settling scores with one
another while Fed watchers score count hawks
and doves. All the while, the big questions for the
economy go unanswered. And the big risks over
the horizon go unaddressed… We should not
encourage the financial markets to be the
handmaiden of the central bank. We should allow
asset prices to be an independent source of
economic insight and discipline.”
- Warsh’s Senate confirmation hearing, April 2026
“I think part of the reason why, after making a
mistake in 2021 and 2022, the mistake was
compounded, is the Fed gives its forward
guidance. The Fed tells the whole world what their
dots are going to be, what their forecasts are going
to be. Well, the Fed's human. Then they hold on to
those forecasts longer than they should. I think if
the Fed were to wait until it gets into a meeting
before making a decision, that incremental
deliberation can keep the central bank from
compounding its errors. I think these are big
changes that are needed.”
